Mostly, yeah. It’s tied to work, or even just thinking about work. Like I’ll be sitting there at home and suddenly I’m back in my head going, “Why did they trust me with senior stuff?” and convincing myself I somehow fooled everyone.

Then I’ll spiral off into comparing myself to other people, which is probably not helpful, but yeah—the main trigger seems to be the job and this extra responsibility.\n\nDoctor: Have these feelings started to interfere with how well you do your job?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah. It’s tied to work, or even just thinking about work. Like I’ll be sitting there at home and suddenly I’m back in my head going, “Why did they trust me with senior stuff?” and convincing myself I somehow fooled everyone.\n\nIt’s not even always something happening right that second… it’s more like I start beating myself up that I’m not really senior material and that I’m going to be found out. Then I’ll spiral off into comparing myself to other people, which is probably not helpful, but yeah—the main trigger seems to be the job and this extra responsibility.\n\nDoctor: Have these feelings started to interfere with how well you do your job?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah. I mean, I’m still showing up and getting the work done, so it’s not like I’ve stopped functioning or anything. But I don’t trust myself as much lately, so I keep double-checking things I normally would’ve just finished and moved on from.\n\nAnd then outside of work… well, I know you asked about the job, but I’ve kind of pulled back from people too. Friends, family. I don’t really feel like being around them when I’m not sure what kind of mood I’m going to be in, or whether my judgment is off. So at work it’s more like I’m slower and second-guessing myself.\n\nDoctor: Before this role change, did you usually feel this same kind of self-doubt in other situations too?\n\nPatient: Not usually in the same way, no. I mean, I’ve had normal doubts before—everybody does, right? Like they gave me more responsibility and I somehow fooled them into thinking I could handle it. And then I start replaying things in my head instead of just doing the work. So before the role change, I don’t think it was this constant or this… personal, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you generally avoided social situations because you worry people will judge or reject you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way. It’s not like I hate those things now. I just don’t really care enough to start.\n\nDoctor: Over the past month, has your appetite changed much?\n\nPatient: Yeah, a bit. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I guess I have. Maybe “worthless” sounds a little strong, but I’ve been beating myself up a lot.

It’s mostly this thing at work, like… since they gave me those senior duties, I keep thinking I’m not actually senior material. Like I somehow fooled everyone into thinking I could handle more than I really can. And then I’ll make one small mistake or not know where something is in the system, and I’ll just replay it in my head like, “See, you shouldn’t be doing this.”

I mean, there’s this old reporting setup nobody really understands anyway, and half the files are named like someone just mashed the keyboard, so maybe it’s not all me, but… it feels like it is. That’s kind of why I came in, honestly. It’s been hard to shut that off.

Then I’ll spiral off into comparing myself to other people, which is probably not helpful, but yeah—the main trigger seems to be the job and this extra responsibility.\n\nDoctor: Have these feelings started to interfere with how well you do your job?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah. I mean, I’m still showing up and getting the work done, so it’s not like I’ve stopped functioning or anything. But I don’t trust myself as much lately, so I keep double-checking things I normally would’ve just finished and moved on from.\n\nAnd then outside of work… well, I know you asked about the job, but I’ve kind of pulled back from people too. Friends, family. I don’t really feel like being around them when I’m not sure what kind of mood I’m going to be in, or whether my judgment is off. So at work it’s more like I’m slower and second-guessing myself.\n\nDoctor: Before this role change, did you usually feel this same kind of self-doubt in other situations too?\n\nPatient: Not usually in the same way, no. I mean, I’ve had normal doubts before—everybody does, right? Like if I’m fixing something at home and I mess it up, I’ll call myself an idiot for five minutes and then move on.\n\nBut this… this is more like I keep coming back to the idea that I’m not really senior material. Like they gave me more responsibility and I somehow fooled them into thinking I could handle it. And then I start replaying things in my head instead of just doing the work. So before the role change, I don’t think it was this constant or this… personal, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you generally avoided social situations because you worry people will judge or reject you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I mean, I don’t love being judged—who does? But I don’t think I’ve generally avoided people because I’m afraid they’ll reject me.\n\nIt’s more… lately with the work stuff, I can get in my own head, and then being around people feels like extra effort. But I don’t know if that’s the same as worrying they’re judging me. I guess I’d have to think about it.\n\nDoctor: Over the past month, have you been feeling down or depressed much of the time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t know why that’s the example that comes to mind—probably because I stare at screens all day—but that’s kind of how it feels. Like the color’s turned down on everything.\n\nDoctor: Over the past month, have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I have. Or I’ll go fishing if the weather’s decent. I’ve got records I like putting on, too, old ones I’ve had forever.\n\nLately, though, the grill just sits there, the fishing stuff is still where I left it, and I’ll look at the records and just… not bother. It’s not like I hate those things now. I just don’t really care enough to start.\n\nDoctor: Over the past month, has your appetite changed much?\n\nPatient: Yeah, a bit. I wouldn’t say it’s some huge thing, but my appetite’s been off. I’ll get home and just grab a few bites of something, or sometimes I’ll skip supper without really deciding to.\n\nIt’s funny, because I used to be pretty regular about dinner—nothing fancy, just whatever’s easy. I have this one pan I always use, and it’s been sitting there clean more often than not, which I guess says something. And my belt’s been a little looser, too. So before the role change, I don’t think it was this constant or this… personal, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you generally avoided social situations because you worry people will judge or reject you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I mean, I don’t love being judged—who does? But I don’t think I’ve generally avoided people because I’m afraid they’ll reject me.\n\nIt’s more… lately with the work stuff, I can get in my own head, and then being around people feels like extra effort. But I don’t know if that’s the same as worrying they’re judging me. I guess I’d have to think about it.\n\nDoctor: Over the past month, have you been feeling down or depressed much of the time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Most days lately there’s been this kind of heavy, low feeling. Not always dramatic, exactly, but like I’m going through work and then coming home and everything’s sort of… muted.\n\nIt reminds me of when you dim a monitor too far and you can still see what’s on the screen, but it’s all washed out. I don’t know why that’s the example that comes to mind—probably because I stare at screens all day—but that’s kind of how it feels. Like the color’s turned down on everything.\n\nDoctor: Over the past month, have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I have. I mean, I usually look forward to little stuff, like grilling on the weekend—messing around with the charcoal, trying not to overcook chicken, that whole thing. Or I’ll go fishing if the weather’s decent. I’ve got records I like putting on, too, old ones I’ve had forever.\n\nLately, though, the grill just sits there, the fishing stuff is still where I left it, and I’ll look at the records and just… not bother. It’s not like I hate those things now. I just don’t really care enough to start.\n\nDoctor: Over the past month, has your appetite changed much?\n\nPatient: Yeah, a bit. I wouldn’t say it’s some huge thing, but my appetite’s been off. I’ll get home and just grab a few bites of something, or sometimes I’ll skip supper without really deciding to.\n\nIt’s funny, because I used to be pretty regular about dinner—nothing fancy, just whatever’s easy. I have this one pan I always use, and it’s been sitting there clean more often than not, which I guess says something. And my belt’s been a little looser, too. I’m not trying to lose weight or anything, it just sort of happened.\n\nDoctor: Did the low mood start after you took on the senior analyst duties?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s about when it became noticeable. After they asked me to cover the senior analyst stuff, maybe around a month ago, I started realizing things were kind of going up and down more.\n\nAt first I thought it was just the workload, you know—new responsibilities, more people asking me questions, all these spreadsheets and old systems nobody documented properly. There’s this one shared folder at work that’s a complete mess, and somehow that became my problem too. But… yeah, the timing lines up. It really started after that.\n\nDoctor: Have these low mood and loss of interest been present for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’d say so. It’s been more than two weeks… probably closer to a month, really. It kind of started getting noticeable after they had me cover the senior analyst duties.\n\nI mean, time’s been a little blurry with work stuff—meetings popping up, people asking where files are, and there’s this one reporting thing that never lines up right—but yeah, the low mood and not caring about things has been around at least a couple weeks.\n\nDoctor: Over the past month, have you been feeling worthless or excessively guilty?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I have. Maybe “worthless” sounds a little strong, but I’ve been beating myself up a lot.\n\nIt’s mostly this thing at work, like… since they gave me those senior duties, I keep thinking I’m not actually senior material. Like I somehow fooled everyone into thinking I could handle more than I really can. And then I’ll make one small mistake or not know where something is in the system, and I’ll just replay it in my head like, “See, you shouldn’t be doing this.”\n\nI mean, there’s this old reporting setup nobody really understands anyway, and half the files are named like someone just mashed the keyboard, so maybe it’s not all me, but… it feels like it is. That’s kind of why I came in, honestly. It’s been hard to shut that off.\n\nDoctor: Have these mood changes been making it hard for you to take care of things in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… a bit. Like if friends or family text, I’ll think, “I should answer that,” and then I just don’t, or I put it off. Part of it is I don’t trust my mood or my judgment right now, and I don’t want to be weird around people. You know, say the wrong thing or make everything awkward.\n\nDoctor: Do your reaction and distress about the new work role feel stronger than you’d expect for the situation?\n\nPatient: Yeah… probably. And then I get stuck on that thought.\n\nIt’s funny, because the title isn’t even officially changed or anything, it’s just duties, but in my head it’s like this whole… proof that I’m going to get exposed. So yeah, I think my reaction is stronger than it probably should be.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts that life isn’t worth living, or thoughts of hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: Um… yeah, a little. I haven’t… I haven’t planned to hurt myself.\n\nBut I do get these thoughts sometimes, like when I’m lying there in the morning before work, or even after a bad day, where it’s like… maybe it would just be easier if I didn’t wake up. Which sounds dramatic when I say it out loud. I don’t know. I don’t want to scare anybody. It’s more like wanting everything to stop for a while than having some specific thing I’m going to do.\n\nDoctor: Over the past month, have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yeah, sleep’s been kind of messed up. Some mornings I wake up way too early and my brain just starts running through client tickets, like stuff I need to fix or what I might’ve missed, and then I can’t get back to sleep.\n\nOther times I technically sleep enough hours, but I still wake up feeling worn out. I haven’t… I haven’t planned to hurt myself.\n\nBut I do get these thoughts sometimes, like when I’m lying there in the morning before work, or even after a bad day, where it’s like… maybe it would just be easier if I didn’t wake up. Which sounds dramatic when I say it out loud. I don’t know. I don’t want to scare anybody. It’s more like wanting everything to stop for a while than having some specific thing I’m going to do.\n\nDoctor: Over the past month, have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yeah, sleep’s been kind of messed up. Some mornings I wake up way too early and my brain just starts running through client tickets, like stuff I need to fix or what I might’ve missed, and then I can’t get back to sleep.\n\nOther times I technically sleep enough hours, but I still wake up feeling worn out. It’s annoying because then I start doing the math, like, “Well, I got seven hours, so why do I feel like this?” But yeah… over the past month, sleep hasn’t felt restful.\n\n[Last Question]\nOver the past month, have you been having trouble concentrating or making decisions?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 43 / computer_systems_analyst / high_school\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m… not totally sure how to answer that. I mean, I feel distracted a lot, but some of that is just the nature of my job too—there’s always some alert or email or someone asking if I can “just take a quick look” at something, and quick look never means quick.

Making decisions… I don’t know. I guess I notice I’ll stare at something longer than I should, like picking what to respond with, but I don’t know if that’s concentration or just me overthinking. My wife would probably say I do that with dinner too, but that’s been true forever, so… yeah, I’m not sure it’s new exactly.

I mean, I’m still showing up and getting the work done, so it’s not like I’ve stopped functioning or anything. But I don’t trust myself as much lately, so I keep double-checking things I normally would’ve just finished and moved on from.\n\nAnd then outside of work… well, I know you asked about the job, but I’ve kind of pulled back from people too. Friends, family. I don’t really feel like being around them when I’m not sure what kind of mood I’m going to be in, or whether my judgment is off. So at work it’s more like I’m slower and second-guessing myself.\n\nDoctor: Before this role change, did you usually feel this same kind of self-doubt in other situations too?\n\nPatient: Not usually in the same way, no. I mean, I’ve had normal doubts before—everybody does, right? Like if I’m fixing something at home and I mess it up, I’ll call myself an idiot for five minutes and then move on.\n\nBut this… this is more like I keep coming back to the idea that I’m not really senior material. Like they gave me more responsibility and I somehow fooled them into thinking I could handle it. And then I start replaying things in my head instead of just doing the work. So before the role change, I don’t think it was this constant or this… personal, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you generally avoided social situations because you worry people will judge or reject you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I mean, I don’t love being judged—who does? But I don’t think I’ve generally avoided people because I’m afraid they’ll reject me.\n\nIt’s more… lately with the work stuff, I can get in my own head, and then being around people feels like extra effort. But I don’t know if that’s the same as worrying they’re judging me. I guess I’d have to think about it.\n\nDoctor: Over the past month, have you been feeling down or depressed much of the time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Most days lately there’s been this kind of heavy, low feeling. Not always dramatic, exactly, but like I’m going through work and then coming home and everything’s sort of… muted.\n\nIt reminds me of when you dim a monitor too far and you can still see what’s on the screen, but it’s all washed out. I don’t know why that’s the example that comes to mind—probably because I stare at screens all day—but that’s kind of how it feels. Like the color’s turned down on everything.\n\nDoctor: Over the past month, have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I have. I mean, I usually look forward to little stuff, like grilling on the weekend—messing around with the charcoal, trying not to overcook chicken, that whole thing. Or I’ll go fishing if the weather’s decent. I’ve got records I like putting on, too, old ones I’ve had forever.\n\nLately, though, the grill just sits there, the fishing stuff is still where I left it, and I’ll look at the records and just… not bother. It’s not like I hate those things now. I just don’t really care enough to start.\n\nDoctor: Over the past month, has your appetite changed much?\n\nPatient: Yeah, a bit. I wouldn’t say it’s some huge thing, but my appetite’s been off. I’ll get home and just grab a few bites of something, or sometimes I’ll skip supper without really deciding to.\n\nIt’s funny, because I used to be pretty regular about dinner—nothing fancy, just whatever’s easy. I have this one pan I always use, and it’s been sitting there clean more often than not, which I guess says something. And my belt’s been a little looser, too. I’m not trying to lose weight or anything, it just sort of happened.\n\nDoctor: Did the low mood start after you took on the senior analyst duties?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s about when it became noticeable. After they asked me to cover the senior analyst stuff, maybe around a month ago, I started realizing things were kind of going up and down more.\n\nAt first I thought it was just the workload, you know—new responsibilities, more people asking me questions, all these spreadsheets and old systems nobody documented properly. There’s this one shared folder at work that’s a complete mess, and somehow that became my problem too. But… yeah, the timing lines up. It really started after that.\n\nDoctor: Have these low mood and loss of interest been present for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’d say so. It’s been more than two weeks… probably closer to a month, really. It kind of started getting noticeable after they had me cover the senior analyst duties.\n\nI mean, time’s been a little blurry with work stuff—meetings popping up, people asking where files are, and there’s this one reporting thing that never lines up right—but yeah, the low mood and not caring about things has been around at least a couple weeks.\n\nDoctor: Over the past month, have you been feeling worthless or excessively guilty?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I have. Maybe “worthless” sounds a little strong, but I’ve been beating myself up a lot.\n\nIt’s mostly this thing at work, like… since they gave me those senior duties, I keep thinking I’m not actually senior material. Like I somehow fooled everyone into thinking I could handle more than I really can. And then I’ll make one small mistake or not know where something is in the system, and I’ll just replay it in my head like, “See, you shouldn’t be doing this.”\n\nI mean, there’s this old reporting setup nobody really understands anyway, and half the files are named like someone just mashed the keyboard, so maybe it’s not all me, but… it feels like it is. That’s kind of why I came in, honestly. It’s been hard to shut that off.\n\nDoctor: Have these mood changes been making it hard for you to take care of things in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… a bit. I mean, I’m still getting to work, so it’s not like everything’s fallen apart or anything. But I’m checking things over and over more than I used to, because I don’t really trust myself lately.\n\nAnd outside of work, I’ve kind of pulled back. Like if friends or family text, I’ll think, “I should answer that,” and then I just don’t, or I put it off. Part of it is I don’t trust my mood or my judgment right now, and I don’t want to be weird around people. You know, say the wrong thing or make everything awkward.\n\nDoctor: Do your reaction and distress about the new work role feel stronger than you’d expect for the situation?\n\nPatient: Yeah… probably. I mean, I know getting new responsibilities is stressful for anybody, so part of me keeps saying, “Well, of course you’re stressed.” But it feels bigger than that.\n\nI came in because I just keep beating myself up, like I’m not really senior material and I somehow fooled everyone into thinking I can handle more than I actually can. And then I get stuck on that thought.\n\nIt’s funny, because the title isn’t even officially changed or anything, it’s just duties, but in my head it’s like this whole… proof that I’m going to get exposed. So yeah, I think my reaction is stronger than it probably should be.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts that life isn’t worth living, or thoughts of hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: Um… yeah, a little. Not like I’ve sat down and made a plan or anything like that. I haven’t… I haven’t planned to hurt myself.\n\nBut I do get these thoughts sometimes, like when I’m lying there in the morning before work, or even after a bad day, where it’s like… maybe it would just be easier if I didn’t wake up. Which sounds dramatic when I say it out loud. I don’t know. I don’t want to scare anybody. It’s more like wanting everything to stop for a while than having some specific thing I’m going to do.\n\nDoctor: Over the past month, have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yeah, sleep’s been kind of messed up. Some mornings I wake up way too early and my brain just starts running through client tickets, like stuff I need to fix or what I might’ve missed, and then I can’t get back to sleep.\n\nOther times I technically sleep enough hours, but I still wake up feeling worn out. It’s annoying because then I start doing the math, like, “Well, I got seven hours, so why do I feel like this?” But yeah… over the past month, sleep hasn’t felt restful.\n\nDoctor: Over the past month, have you been having trouble concentrating or making decisions?\n\nPatient: I’m… not totally sure how to answer that. I mean, I feel distracted a lot, but some of that is just the nature of my job too—there’s always some alert or email or someone asking if I can “just take a quick look” at something, and quick look never means quick.\n\nMaking decisions… I don’t know. Most days lately there’s been this kind of heavy, low feeling. Not always dramatic, exactly, but like I’m going through work and then coming home and everything’s sort of… muted.\n\nIt reminds me of when you dim a monitor too far and you can still see what’s on the screen, but it’s all washed out. I don’t know why that’s the example that comes to mind—probably because I stare at screens all day—but that’s kind of how it feels. Like the color’s turned down on everything.\n\nDoctor: Over the past month, have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I have. I mean, I usually look forward to little stuff, like grilling on the weekend—messing around with the charcoal, trying not to overcook chicken, that whole thing. Or I’ll go fishing if the weather’s decent. I’ve got records I like putting on, too, old ones I’ve had forever.\n\nLately, though, the grill just sits there, the fishing stuff is still where I left it, and I’ll look at the records and just… not bother. It’s not like I hate those things now. I just don’t really care enough to start.\n\nDoctor: Over the past month, has your appetite changed much?\n\nPatient: Yeah, a bit. I wouldn’t say it’s some huge thing, but my appetite’s been off. I’ll get home and just grab a few bites of something, or sometimes I’ll skip supper without really deciding to.\n\nIt’s funny, because I used to be pretty regular about dinner—nothing fancy, just whatever’s easy. I have this one pan I always use, and it’s been sitting there clean more often than not, which I guess says something. And my belt’s been a little looser, too. I’m not trying to lose weight or anything, it just sort of happened.\n\nDoctor: Did the low mood start after you took on the senior analyst duties?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s about when it became noticeable. After they asked me to cover the senior analyst stuff, maybe around a month ago, I started realizing things were kind of going up and down more.\n\nAt first I thought it was just the workload, you know—new responsibilities, more people asking me questions, all these spreadsheets and old systems nobody documented properly. There’s this one shared folder at work that’s a complete mess, and somehow that became my problem too. But… yeah, the timing lines up. It really started after that.\n\nDoctor: Have these low mood and loss of interest been present for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’d say so. It’s been more than two weeks… probably closer to a month, really. It kind of started getting noticeable after they had me cover the senior analyst duties.\n\nI mean, time’s been a little blurry with work stuff—meetings popping up, people asking where files are, and there’s this one reporting thing that never lines up right—but yeah, the low mood and not caring about things has been around at least a couple weeks.\n\nDoctor: Over the past month, have you been feeling worthless or excessively guilty?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I have. Maybe “worthless” sounds a little strong, but I’ve been beating myself up a lot.\n\nIt’s mostly this thing at work, like… since they gave me those senior duties, I keep thinking I’m not actually senior material. Like I somehow fooled everyone into thinking I could handle more than I really can. And then I’ll make one small mistake or not know where something is in the system, and I’ll just replay it in my head like, “See, you shouldn’t be doing this.”\n\nI mean, there’s this old reporting setup nobody really understands anyway, and half the files are named like someone just mashed the keyboard, so maybe it’s not all me, but… it feels like it is. That’s kind of why I came in, honestly. It’s been hard to shut that off.\n\nDoctor: Have these mood changes been making it hard for you to take care of things in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… a bit. I mean, I’m still getting to work, so it’s not like everything’s fallen apart or anything. But I’m checking things over and over more than I used to, because I don’t really trust myself lately.\n\nAnd outside of work, I’ve kind of pulled back. Like if friends or family text, I’ll think, “I should answer that,” and then I just don’t, or I put it off. Part of it is I don’t trust my mood or my judgment right now, and I don’t want to be weird around people. You know, say the wrong thing or make everything awkward.\n\nDoctor: Do your reaction and distress about the new work role feel stronger than you’d expect for the situation?\n\nPatient: Yeah… probably. I mean, I know getting new responsibilities is stressful for anybody, so part of me keeps saying, “Well, of course you’re stressed.” But it feels bigger than that.\n\nI came in because I just keep beating myself up, like I’m not really senior material and I somehow fooled everyone into thinking I can handle more than I actually can. And then I get stuck on that thought.\n\nIt’s funny, because the title isn’t even officially changed or anything, it’s just duties, but in my head it’s like this whole… proof that I’m going to get exposed. So yeah, I think my reaction is stronger than it probably should be.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts that life isn’t worth living, or thoughts of hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: Um… yeah, a little. Not like I’ve sat down and made a plan or anything like that. I haven’t… I haven’t planned to hurt myself.\n\nBut I do get these thoughts sometimes, like when I’m lying there in the morning before work, or even after a bad day, where it’s like… maybe it would just be easier if I didn’t wake up. Which sounds dramatic when I say it out loud. I don’t know. I don’t want to scare anybody. It’s more like wanting everything to stop for a while than having some specific thing I’m going to do.\n\nDoctor: Over the past month, have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yeah, sleep’s been kind of messed up. Some mornings I wake up way too early and my brain just starts running through client tickets, like stuff I need to fix or what I might’ve missed, and then I can’t get back to sleep.\n\nOther times I technically sleep enough hours, but I still wake up feeling worn out. It’s annoying because then I start doing the math, like, “Well, I got seven hours, so why do I feel like this?” But yeah… over the past month, sleep hasn’t felt restful.\n\nDoctor: Over the past month, have you been having trouble concentrating or making decisions?\n\nPatient: I’m… not totally sure how to answer that. I mean, I feel distracted a lot, but some of that is just the nature of my job too—there’s always some alert or email or someone asking if I can “just take a quick look” at something, and quick look never means quick.\n\nMaking decisions… I don’t know. I guess I notice I’ll stare at something longer than I should, like picking what to respond with, but I don’t know if that’s concentration or just me overthinking. My wife would probably say I do that with dinner too, but that’s been true forever, so… yeah, I’m not sure it’s new exactly.\n\nDoctor: Over the past month, have you been moving or speaking noticeably slower than usual, or feeling so restless that you can’t sit still?\n\nPatient: Not slower, I don’t think. If anything it’s kind of the opposite sometimes. Like after work, I’ll get home and I can’t just sit on the couch like a normal person. I start checking servers again, or I’ll notice some little thing around the house and suddenly I’m fixing that, then I’m in the garage looking for a tool, then I remember another thing.\n\nIt’s not always productive exactly, it just feels like everything has to be handled right then. My wife will be like, “Can you just sit down for a minute?” and I’m like, yeah, I can, but then I’m up again checking something stupid like whether an update finished. So… restless, yeah, I guess that fits more than moving slow.\n\nDoctor: Even when you're not thinking about work, do you still feel down?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, work definitely sets me off because there’s always something broken or about to be broken, but even when I’m not actively thinking about that, there’s still this kind of heavy, low feeling.\n\nLike I’ll be home doing normal stuff, and it’s like everything’s just… dulled down. Not dramatic exactly, just most days lately it feels like I’m moving through work and home with the color turned down.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
